Itâs time to get to work&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum-eo-2026&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;The new executive order sets a 2030 migration deadline and establishes a powerful foundation for post-quantum resilience. We look at what it gets right, where it can go further, and our migration playbook for government and industry.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;On June 22, 2026, President Trump signed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Executive Order 14412&lt;/a&gt;, &amp;quot;Securing the Nation Against Advanced Cryptographic Attacks.&amp;quot; The order sets a December 31, 2030, deadline for federal agencies to transition their most sensitive systems to &lt;i&gt;post-quantum encryption&lt;/i&gt;, and a December 31, 2031, deadline for &lt;i&gt;post-quantum authentication&lt;/i&gt;. The EO also directs federal contractors to comply with post-quantum Federal Information Processing Standards (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/post-quantum-cryptography\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;FIPS&lt;/a&gt;) by the end of 2030.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We welcome this executive order. The U.S. government has a long track record of using federal leadership and procurement to drive adoption of new technologies across the broader industry. We&amp;#39;ve seen this work with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://obamawhitehouse.archives.gov/sites/default/files/omb/assets/egov_docs/transition-to-ipv6.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;IPv6&lt;/a&gt;, with routing security and the Resource Public Key Infrastructure (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/189/final\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;RPKI&lt;/a&gt;), and with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://obamawhitehouse.archives.gov/sites/default/files/omb/memoranda/fy2008/m08-23.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;DNSSEC&lt;/a&gt;, and weâre glad to see this tradition continue with post-quantum cryptography.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The EO is especially important at this moment because the timeline for &lt;i&gt;Q-Day&lt;/i&gt;, the day that quantum computers can &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/the-quantum-menace/#shors-algorithm\&quot;&gt;break&lt;/a&gt; the public-key cryptography used across the Internet, has been accelerated. In April 2026, Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-roadmap/\&quot;&gt;moved our own target for full post-quantum security to 2029&lt;/a&gt;, following research breakthroughs from &lt;a href=\&quot;https://research.google/blog/safeguarding-cryptocurrency-by-disclosing-quantum-vulnerabilities-responsibly/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Google&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Oratomic&lt;/a&gt;. This EO updates guidance from 2024, when the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) &lt;a href=\&quot;https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2024/NIST.IR.8547.ipd.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;stated&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that the classical public key cryptography used across the Internet (namely RSA and Elliptic Curve Cryptography, which can be broken once powerful quantum computers become available) should be deprecated by 2030 and disallowed by 2035.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The Internetâs transition to post-quantum encryption is well underway, while the transition to post-quantum authentication has only just begun. Today, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/post-quantum\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;over two-thirds&lt;/a&gt; of browser traffic to Cloudflare&amp;#39;s network is protected with post-quantum encryption, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/pqc-cloudflare-products/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;most of our products&lt;/a&gt; support post-quantum key agreement. Our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-sase/\&quot;&gt;SASE platform, Cloudflare One&lt;/a&gt;, provides post-quantum encryption across all major on-ramps and off-ramps, including &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-zero-trust/\&quot;&gt;TLS&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-warp/\&quot;&gt;MASQUE&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-ipsec/\&quot;&gt;IPsec&lt;/a&gt;. We&amp;#39;ve recently started &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/bootstrap-mtc/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;deploying&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post-quantum authentication and aim to be fully post-quantum secure by 2029. The EO is an excellent foundation and builds on work from the previous two Administrations. We&amp;#39;ve been doing the work the EO is asking federal agencies to do &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/the-tls-post-quantum-experiment/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;since 2019&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, we have some thoughts on what the order gets right, we see opportunities for the Office of Management and Budget (OMB) to strengthen and facilitate cost-effective agency migration, and we provide a roadmap for how organizations and agencies can advance their transition most effectively.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-eos-requirements-for-federal-systems\&quot;&gt;The EOâs requirements for federal systems&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-eos-requirements-for-federal-systems\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The bulk of the EO&amp;#39;s binding requirements are aimed at two categories of federal systems: High Value Assets (HVAs) and high impact systems. HVAs are federal information or systems &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2018/12/M-19-03.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;designated by OMB&lt;/a&gt; as the government&amp;#39;s crown jewels: systems whose compromise would significantly affect national security, foreign relations, or public confidence. These include databases that hold millions of federal employee records, systems that process classified intelligence, or platforms that manage federal financial transactions. Meanwhile, high impact systems are those where confidentiality, integrity, or availability is rated &amp;quot;high&amp;quot; under &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/199/final\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;FIPS 199&lt;/a&gt;, meaning a breach could cause severe harm including loss of life, major financial damage, or significant degradation of an agency&amp;#39;s ability to carry out its mission.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The EO has the power to bind federal agencies, but not other organizations (i.e., critical infrastructure, state, local, tribal and territorial governments, academia, civil society). Thatâs why the EO only gives these deadlines to federal agencies:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;table&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Date&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Requirement&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;July 2026&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Each federal agency head identifies a PQC migration lead and provides their name and contact details to OMB and the National Cyber Director.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;September 2026&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;OMB issues guidance requiring each agency to: (1) review their inventory of HVAs and high impact systems; (2) plan for PQC migration; and (3) submit that plan to OMB and the National Cyber Director.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;December 2030&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;All HVAs and high impact systems must be transitioned to PQC for key establishment.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;December 2031&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;All HVAs and high impact systems must be transitioned to PQC for digital signatures.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;/table&gt;&lt;p&gt;National Security Systems are explicitly excluded from these deadlines. They are on a separate, classified track managed by the NSA with deadlines between 2030 and 2033 &lt;a href=\&quot;https://media.defense.gov/2025/May/30/2003728741/-1/-1/0/CSA_CNSA_2.0_ALGORITHMS.PDF\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;already set in 2022&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;two-migrations-encryption-and-authentication-both-should-begin-now\&quot;&gt;Two migrations: encryption and authentication. Both should begin now.&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#two-migrations-encryption-and-authentication-both-should-begin-now\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The EO splits the PQC migration into two phases: post-quantum key establishment (encryption) by 2030, and post-quantum digital signatures and certificates (authentication) by 2031. This accurately reflects the availability of post-quantum encryption across the Internet today. Our own &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-roadmap/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;deadline&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for full post-quantum readiness (including authentication) is 2029, but we are amongst the earliest adopters in the industry.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We are also happy to see the EO focusing on &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/post-quantum-cryptography\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;NIST-standardized post-quantum cryptographic algorithms&lt;/a&gt; and not Quantum Key Distribution (QKD), since QKD &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/you-dont-need-quantum-hardware/\&quot;&gt;does not operate at Internet scale&lt;/a&gt; due to its need for specialized hardware and dedicated physical links between sender and receiver.Â Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now letâs have a deeper look at the two migrations called for and required in the EO: post-quantum encryption and post-quantum authentication.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Post-quantum encryption&lt;/b&gt; is needed today to stop &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_now,_decrypt_later\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;harvest-now-decrypt-later attacks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, where an adversary collects encrypted traffic today and decrypts it later once quantum computers are powerful enough. Post-quantum encryption is especially valuable for organizations handling data that will still have value to adversaries 3-10 years from now, like government agencies, banks, healthcare organizations, defense contractors, and telecom providers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Post-quantum authentication &lt;/b&gt;stops an adversary that has a quantum computer from forging certificates to impersonate servers, generating malicious code signatures, or gaining unauthorized access to systems.Â  Post-quantum authentication is needed only after Q-Day risk materializes, because it stops attacks that are possible only once a cryptographically-relevant quantum computer (CRQC) exists. &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs important to put the migration timelines in context with advancements in quantum computing. In addition to yesterdayâs EO on post-quantum security, President Trump also signed an &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/ushering-in-the-next-frontier-of-quantum-innovation/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;EO&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to accelerate deployment and commercialization of quantum computing, sensing, and networking. The fact that the EO sets a 2031 deadline for post-quantum authentication tells us something important: the U.S. government believes there is a non-negligible chance that a CRQC could be operational around that time.  &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;road-to-quantum-safety\&quot;&gt;Road to Quantum Safety&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#road-to-quantum-safety\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4ZPdZZj0jIz2ZFsgOdTLnW/b3633cb8438622a4af0bba1ad63c799d/Screenshot_2026-06-29_at_12.23.04.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3360 Screenshot 2026-06-29 at 11.43.52\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;2054\&quot; height=\&quot;1014\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;What about the state of these two technologies? The migration to post-quantum authentication is a bigger challenge than post-quantum encryption for a few reasons, including:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Post-quantum &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;ML-DSA&lt;/a&gt; digital signatures are larger than classic digital signatures, which could have an impact on performance of some systems, for instance in short-lived TLS connections. Thatâs why we are working with Google Chrome on &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/bootstrap-mtc/\&quot;&gt;Merkle Tree Certificates&lt;/a&gt; to solve the performance problem for TLS.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The dependency chain for post-quantum authentication is longer, requiring coordinated upgrades across clients, servers, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://letsencrypt.org/2026/06/03/pq-certs\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;certificate authorities&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/azul-certificate-transparency-log/\&quot;&gt;certificate transparency logs&lt;/a&gt;, root stores, and browsers.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;There is only limited ecosystem deployment of post-quantum authentication so far, as compared to the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/post-quantum\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;much broader deployment&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; of post-quantum encryption.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;It is interesting that the EO sets a one-year gap between the encryption and authentication deadlines. One extra year of calendar time is tight, so this work cannot proceed sequentially. The ecosystem needs to start working on both of these targets concurrently, or we will miss this 2031 deadline.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cryptographic deployment across the Internet cannot happen without standards developed by the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.ietf.org/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Internet Engineering Task Force&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (IETF). They are working to transition their protocols to post-quantum cryptography.Â  The TLS community is ahead, with the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/plants/about/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;IETF PLANTS working group&lt;/a&gt; making good progress on post-quantum certificates for TLS. There is much work to do here, and we look forward to supporting the IETF in its efforts.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;supply-chain-pressure-that-helps-everyone\&quot;&gt;Supply chain pressure that helps everyone&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#supply-chain-pressure-that-helps-everyone\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The EO includes requirements for federal contractors, which may turn out to be the most impactful part of the EO.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Namely, the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.acquisition.gov/far-council-members\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;FAR Council&lt;/a&gt; must publish proposed rules requiring &amp;quot;covered contractors&amp;quot; to comply with NIST FIPS incorporating PQC algorithms by December 31, 2030 (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 6(c)&lt;/a&gt;). The FAR Council must also publish proposed rules requiring contractors to implement vulnerability disclosure programs that cover cryptographic vulnerabilities (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 6(d)&lt;/a&gt;). These proposed rules need to go through notice-and-comment rulemaking, but the EO has a December 31, 2030, target which is still important. This deadline is one year earlier than federal agencies are required to complete their post-quantum authentication migration, so that federal contractors will be ready before agencies hit their own deadlines.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Federal agencies can only migrate to PQC if the products they buy support PQC. To put this into practice, CISA &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/product-categories-technologies-use-post-quantum-cryptography-standards\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;released&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; its &lt;i&gt;Product Categories for Technologies That Use Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards&lt;/i&gt;, drawing a clear line between technologies where PQC is already &amp;quot;widely available&amp;quot; versus those still &amp;quot;transitioning.&amp;quot; The &amp;quot;widely available&amp;quot; list includes cloud platforms (IaaS, PaaS), web browsers and servers, chat and messaging software, and endpoint security products like full disk encryption. For these categories, CISA&amp;#39;s guidance is clear: organizations should procure only PQC-capable products. The &amp;quot;transitioning&amp;quot; list, where PQC is not yet widely available, includes networking hardware (routers, firewalls, switches), identity and access management systems (HSMs, certificate authorities, identity providers), email servers and clients, and database systems.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;By telling contractors their products must be PQC-compliant by 2030, and directing agencies to immediately favor PQC-capable vendors in mature markets, the federal framework forces the vendor ecosystem to ship PQC-capable products on a fixed timeline. Products that vendors build to federal requirements will end up used by hospitals, banks, universities, and small businesses, which makes PQC support more broadly available. Cloudflare is among the many vendors subject to these requirements, and because networking software and cloud services are already designated by CISA as widely available PQC categories, we&amp;#39;ve already shipped post-quantum encryption across &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/pqc-cloudflare-products/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;most of our products&lt;/a&gt; at &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-crypto-should-be-free/\&quot;&gt;no extra cost&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;critical-infrastructure-and-pq-for-everyone\&quot;&gt;Critical infrastructure and PQ for everyone&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#critical-infrastructure-and-pq-for-everyone\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The EO also speaks to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/5195c\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;critical infrastructure&lt;/a&gt;: energy, financial services, water, transportation, telecommunications, healthcare, and other systems whose failure would have a serious or significant impact on the country. While the EO has no hard migration deadline for critical infrastructure owners and operators, the EO directs certain federal agencies to &amp;quot;assist&amp;quot; critical infrastructure owners and operators with their PQC migration plans (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 5(a)&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;While the EO focuses mostly on federal agencies and critical infrastructure in the U.S., post-quantum cryptography is important to every Internet-connected individual and organization. Harvest-now-decrypt-later attacks are a risk today. And after Q-Day, the risk of unauthorized access by an adversary armed with a quantum computer will impact any organization, big or small. When we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;launched free universal SSL in 2014&lt;/a&gt;, our CEO Matthew Prince wrote:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Having cutting-edge encryption may not seem important to a small blog, but it is critical to advancing the encrypted-by-default future of the Internet. Every byte, however seemingly mundane, that flows encrypted across the Internet makes it more difficult for those who wish to intercept, throttle, or censor the web.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;We feel the same way about post-quantum cryptography. Thatâs why every post-quantum upgrade we build is available to all customers, on every plan, at no additional cost.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/399EKuhN38jLx1pAGEgx3z/1f93784f4c98df9a108b5c8d1803218b/BLOG-3360_3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3360 3\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1287\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;opportunities-for-ombs-implementation-guidance\&quot;&gt;Opportunities for OMBâs implementation guidance&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#opportunities-for-ombs-implementation-guidance\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The EO sets the direction, and now OMB has 90 days to provide important clarifications and operational guidance to achieve the most effective PQC migration across federal agencies (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 4(b)&lt;/a&gt;). Based on what we&amp;#39;ve learned from our own PQC migration, here are a few elements that we suggest that guidance should include:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Define what it means to âtransition.â &lt;/b&gt;The EO requires agencies to &amp;quot;transition&amp;quot; their systems to PQC, but it never defines what &amp;quot;transition&amp;quot; means. Does it mean the system supports PQC algorithms? That it prefers them? Or that classical cryptography has been disabled entirely?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;These are very different security postures. A system that supports ML-KEM but still allows a classical-only TLS handshake is vulnerable to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downgrade_attack\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;downgrade attacks&lt;/a&gt;. An adversary capable of intercepting traffic could force the connection back to classical key exchange. The system would have &amp;quot;transitioned&amp;quot; to PQC in name, but still be vulnerable to the same quantum attacks the order is trying to prevent.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;History is instructive. When SSLv3 was deprecated after the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/padding-oracles-and-the-decline-of-cbc-mode-ciphersuites/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;POODLE attack&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2014, servers kept SSLv3 enabled for backwards compatibility, allowing attackers to force connections to downgrade and then exploit SSLv3&amp;#39;s weaknesses. It took years for the ecosystem to actually turn SSLv3 off. To avoid repeating this pattern, we need a clear definition of âdoneâ that includes disabling quantum-vulnerable cryptography to prevent downgrades.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Crypto agility&lt;/b&gt;: &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptographic_agility\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Crypto agility&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is the ability to swap cryptographic algorithms without re-architecting your systems. The EO mandates migrating to specific NIST crypto standards, but says nothing about building systems that can swap cryptographic algorithms if these algorithms need to change in the future. Crypto agility doesn&amp;#39;t mean supporting every algorithm at once. It means building systems so that when the community converges on a better algorithm in the future, the upgrade is a configuration change, not a re-architecture. The OMB should include this in its guidance.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;CBOM or quantum impact inventory? &lt;/b&gt;The EO directs CISA and NIST to publish guidance on the minimum elements for a cryptographic bill of materials (CBOM) within 270 days (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 5(d)&lt;/a&gt;). A CBOM is an inventory of the cryptographic algorithms, protocols, and implementations used in a given hardware or software product, similar to a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cisa.gov/sbom\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;software bill of materials (SBOM)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In theory, CBOMs are a good idea. In practice, we&amp;#39;d caution against treating exhaustive cryptographic inventories as a prerequisite for action. A detailed CBOM of every algorithm in every library in every product takes a long time to produce, it can take federal agencies an entire procurement cycle of discovery tooling and consulting, and it potentially becomes stale by the time the inventory is complete. Also, a CBOM doesnât list systems that should be using cryptography but are not. And a CBOM lists keys without an &lt;a href=\&quot;https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22442\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;understanding of their purpose&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, making them less useful for organizations trying to understand the risk associated with a quantum-vulnerable key. &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We think that a quantum impact inventory is a more productive framing. What would be the impact if the system or its data is compromised? How likely is that to happen? What measures can be taken to mitigate the risk, whether a drop-in replacement, a software update, or a compensating control like tunneling traffic over bulk post-quantum connection or isolating it from the Internet? How feasible is each option and what dependency chain does it create? Identifying these informs where to take action first. You can fill in the details of a full CBOM over time if that makes sense for your organization, but you should start by discovering your most exposed and impactful systems.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Making post-quantum cryptography affordable to all.&lt;/b&gt; True national resilience fails if post-quantum cryptography is treated as a gated luxury rather than a universal baseline. OMB policy must resist vendor lock-in or toll booths that leave underfunded critical infrastructure behind or increase technical debt at federal agencies.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;what-to-do-now-dont-wait-for-2030\&quot;&gt;What to do now: don&amp;#39;t wait for 2030&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-to-do-now-dont-wait-for-2030\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;You do not have to wait for 2030 or an exhaustive cryptographic inventory to start your migration. History has shown that updating cryptography is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/sha-1-deprecation-no-browser-left-behind/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;hard&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and can take a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-131Ar2.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;long&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/radius-udp-vulnerable-md5-attack/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;time&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;; other organizations should start sorting out their migrations as well. So as we wait for OMB guidance for federal agencies, hereâs what we recommend for all organizations:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Protect your Internet traffic now.&lt;/b&gt; Start with traffic that crosses the public Internet, because that is the easiest for adversaries to harvest now and the most immediately at risk. If your web traffic flows through Cloudflare, your connections are largely protected with post-quantum encryption. If your enterprise network uses &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-sase/\&quot;&gt;Cloudflare One&lt;/a&gt;, your private network traffic is also protected. If your provider doesn&amp;#39;t support post-quantum encryption, switch to one that does. Even if the individual applications running inside your network haven&amp;#39;t been upgraded yet, start &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-warp/\&quot;&gt;tunneling your traffic&lt;/a&gt; through post-quantum encrypted infrastructure to protect it in bulk, even if individual systems are not yet inventoried and upgraded.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Update procurement.&lt;/b&gt; Make &amp;quot;post-quantum encryption by default, at no additional cost, with a clear roadmap for post-quantum authentication and crypto agility&amp;quot; a requirement in every technology procurement. If your vendor charges extra for post-quantum security or doesn&amp;#39;t have a roadmap or plan, ask why or find another vendor.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Quantum impact inventory.&lt;/b&gt; For traffic that stays inside your private network perimeter and is not exposed to the public Internet, the harvest-now-decrypt-later risk is lower because an adversary would need to be on your network to capture it. But you still need to know what cryptography your internal systems use, so you can plan your migration. Use a &lt;i&gt;quantum impact inventory &lt;/i&gt;as a tool to prioritize your efforts, for example focusing on systems or connections that handle sensitive data or are exposed on the public Internet.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Plan for authentication now.&lt;/b&gt; The 2031 deadline for post-quantum authentication will come faster than you think. Start identifying your long-lived keys, root certificates, and code-signing infrastructure. These are the highest-priority targets for a quantum attacker, and they have the longest dependency chains to upgrade. Now is a great time to update your software libraries and automate certificate provisioning even if post-quantum certificates are not yet available in your ecosystem. And make sure your vendors are planning to be ready for the looming post-quantum authentication deadline.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;aligning-policy-and-international-standards\&quot;&gt;Aligning policy and international standards&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#aligning-policy-and-international-standards\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;At the same time, work should also start now on aligning global government policy with international standards. We were glad to see that Section 5(b) directs the State Department to engage foreign governments and industry groups to encourage adoption of NIST-standardized PQC algorithms.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hereâs why this matters. Cryptography migrations cannot be run in a vacuum, with each country operating within its own borders. A TLS connection between a U.S. person and a server abroad only works if both ends negotiate the same cryptography. NIST has been running open international cryptographic competitions for decades. The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/cryptographic-standards-and-guidelines/archived-crypto-projects/aes-development\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;AES competition&lt;/a&gt; (1997-2001) produced the encryption standard used across the Internet today, selecting a cipher designed by Belgian cryptographers. The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/hash-functions/sha-3-project\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;SHA-3 competition&lt;/a&gt; (2007-2012) produced the latest hash standard, selecting an algorithm designed by a Belgian-Italian team. The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/post-quantum-cryptography\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;PQC competition&lt;/a&gt; (2016-2024) followed the same open model: anyone could submit, anyone could analyze, and the winning algorithms were designed by international teams. ML-KEM, the key agreement standard now being deployed across the Internet, was created largely by European cryptographers. These are open, internationally vetted algorithms. NIST organized the competitions, but the results belong to the global cryptographic community.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The risk ahead is fragmentation. If different jurisdictions mandate different algorithms, the result is cipher bloat and increased attack surface: more code to write, test, and audit, more surface for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downgrade_attack\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;downgrade attacks&lt;/a&gt;, and slower deployment for everyone. We&amp;#39;ve &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-sase/#but-what-about-interoperability\&quot;&gt;seen this happen&lt;/a&gt; firsthand in IPsec, where the lack of an interoperable standard led vendors to ship proprietary PQ key agreement algorithms that couldnât interoperate, delaying the migration by years. The TLS community went the opposite way, converging on a single hybrid key agreement (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-tls-ecdhe-mlkem/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;X25519MLKEM768&lt;/a&gt;), and deployment followed quickly.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We are big fans of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.nist.gov/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;NIST&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and especially its leadership in vetting standards globally and standardizing cryptography worldwide. We encourage the Trump Administration to work with Congress to ensure that NIST has appropriate resources, staffing, and tooling to meet current and emerging deliverables in this EO and others, like America&amp;#39;s &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/Americas-AI-Action-Plan.pdf\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AI Action Plan&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We&amp;#39;d like to see State Department-led engagement drive real alignment: adoption of the same NIST algorithms across allied nations, alignment on timelines, and mutual recognition of cryptographic algorithms and modules. The Internet is one network, and its cryptography should be one standard.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;speeding-up-cmvp\&quot;&gt;Speeding up CMVP&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#speeding-up-cmvp\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;As a final note, the EO directs NIST to revise the processes used by the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/Projects/cryptographic-module-validation-program\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Cryptographic Module Validation Program (CMVP)&lt;/a&gt; to accelerate validations of cryptographic modules (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/06/securing-the-nation-against-advanced-cryptographic-attacks/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;Sec. 6(b)&lt;/a&gt;). Having bumped up against the CMVP program for years, we are extremely happy to see this in the order.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;CMVP exists for a good reason. Federal agencies and their contractors need a way to verify that the cryptography inside a product actually does what it claims: that AES is implemented correctly or that random number generators have enough entropy. CMVP has been tuned for a steady state where cryptography doesnât change much.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Going forward, CMVP needs to be adjusted to accept the realities of the impending migration. We welcome the &lt;i&gt;FedRAMP update stream&lt;/i&gt; that allows updated modules to be used immediately before final validation. This allows faster adoption of post-quantum cryptography, and correction of implementation errors that were missed in validation. Similar allowances for CMVP are essential.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;go-forth-and-pq-all-the-things\&quot;&gt;Go forth and PQ all the things&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#go-forth-and-pq-all-the-things\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;This post-quantum EO is a meaningful step. It sets real deadlines and creates supply chain pressure that will accelerate adoption across the industry.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For organizations starting their own migration, we suggest you start by protecting your public Internet traffic along with updates to your procurement requirements, followed by a quantum impact inventory to figure out where to focus next. Do not let cryptography inventory slow you down from deploying post-quantum encryption across your most sensitive systems immediately.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cryptographic deployment across the Internet depends on standards developed by the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.ietf.org/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;IETF&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. The TLS community &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/wg/lamps/about/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;is&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/plants/about/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;further along&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, but there is lots more work to do across other protocol communities, and we look forward to supporting those efforts.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Let us go forth and PQ all the things, quickly and together. Free TLS helped encrypt the web. Free post-quantum cryptography will help secure it for what comes next.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;You can get started now on Cloudflare by visiting our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/pqc/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;PQC page&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-06-23T18:25:18.163Z&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-06-29T13:03:46.869Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/sF5WBgsztnfV2hP4btNVD/22f810e0a5163f45277301b868dbac66/BLOG-3360_1.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6bIo7ayy56Fzdrtf9z2EWy&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Post-Quantum&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6Mp7ouACN2rT3YjL1xaXJx&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Security&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;security&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;1QsJUMpv0QBSLiVZLLQJ3V&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Cryptography&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;cryptography&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;16yk8DVbNNifxov5cWvAov&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Policy &amp;
